你在 Windows 上工作，工作目录 `D:\zynq-yolo`（Zynq-7020 足球判罚辅助系统，AMD FPGA 创新赛道，最终要落到 PL 逻辑里跑）。任务：**验证「检测球场白线（边线/球门线/中线）、中圈与罚球弧、以及球门」这条路可行不可行**，并用实测数据给出结论。

## 为什么这件事重要
判罚系统需要一个**绝对参考系**：判断球是否出界要球门线/边线，判断越位要中线与最后一名防守球员，判断是否在禁区内要禁区线。没有场地几何，任何"球在画面里偏左"都是没有意义的描述。所以这些线是判罚的"尺子"。

## 可用资源（本机实测事实）
- 40 张标定帧：`data\samples\calib_real\frames_calib\`（30 张）+ `frames_test\`（10 张），640×360 JPEG；时间戳在 `data\samples\calib_real\MANIFEST.csv` 的 `t_approx_s` 列（列首 `split`）。
- 源视频：`D:\yt-dlp\downloads\migu_5106947698\migu_5106947698.mp4`（1280x720，25 fps，8518 s）—— **第三方转播内容，绝对不得复制进仓库，只能读**。ffmpeg 在 `D:\yt-dlp\ffmpeg\ffmpeg.exe`（**无 ffprobe**）。
- Python `D:\python\python.exe`（3.13.1）：`numpy 2.5.4`、`opencv-python 5.0.0.93`（`cv2` 含 `HoughLinesP`/`HoughCircles`/`fitEllipse`/`goodFeaturesToTrack`）、`scipy 1.18.1`、`ultralytics 8.4.182`、`torch 2.14.1+cpu`（**纯 CPU**）。**禁止 pip install / npm install**。
- 可复用（**只读，不要改**）：`tools\pynq_net.py`（`load_image_input` 等）、`tools\eval_calib_set.py`（`list_images`）、`tools\draw_detections.py`（**渲染配色沿用这套**：绿=float32、红=INT8、品红=EXTRA、橙=MISS、黄=次阈值）、`tools\make_calib_from_video.py`（关键帧抽取，含 `green_ratio`，可直接借它的草坪判定）。

## 你要做的
1. **白线分割**：草坪是绿色大面积背景，白线是高亮度低饱和度的细长结构。设计一个对**光照变化（有白天/阴天/夜间三类帧）**稳健的分割（提示：在 HSV/Lab 里做相对阈值，而不是固定亮度阈值；可考虑"局部对比度"而非全局）。注意干扰项：**白色球衣、白色广告牌、球门网、球员的白袜子、边裁旗**。你必须把这些当成真实的假阳性来调试，而不是假设它们不存在。
2. **直线检测与归类**：用 Hough 类方法找长直线，再把直线归成「边线／球门线／中线／禁区线／罚球区线」。归类必须有依据（用直线的位置与相互几何关系，如近端边线与远端边线在像面上近似平行、球门线与边线近似垂直等）。**不要把"找到了一条直线"当成找到边线。**
3. **中圈 / 罚球弧**：尝试检测圆弧（椭圆在透视下）。如果做不到，如实说明为什么（例如分辨率下弧线只有十几像素、被球员遮挡）——**允许失败，但必须有具体理由和实测证据**。
4. **球门**：检测白色立柱与横梁（在部分帧里球门可见）。给出门框被检出的帧数与位置。
5. **输出人眼可复核的图**：分割出的线条掩膜、检出的直线/弧（不同类别不同颜色）、以及门框，全部画到原图上，存 `data\results\viz\pitch\`（该目录已在 `.gitignore` 里，不入库）。**我会亲自看图判断"这是不是真的边线"。**

## 判据（必须先能失败，不接受"看起来还行"）
- **主判据：几何自洽性，而不是"检出了多少条线"。** 对每帧，取检出的两条边线（近端/远端）与球门线，做一致性检查：球场是一个矩形，在像面上四条线应交于合理的消失点，且**边线与球门线在近地平面上的夹角应接近 90°**。报告每帧的自洽性残差与通过帧数。**只看"线长 > N 像素"是不够的判据，那样广告牌边缘也会通过。**
- **必须做的负对照（这条最关键，直接决定结论有效性）**：
  a) **非球场区域对照**：把图像上半部分（看台/观众/广告）裁出来跑同一套检测，如果照样"检出边线"，说明判据没有判别力，必须如实报告；
  b) **打乱/翻转对照**：把图像做水平翻转或加噪声后重跑，自洽性通过率不应显著上升；
  c) **参数敏感性**：至少扫 3 组关键阈值，报告通过帧数如何变化。如果结果对阈值极端敏感，说明不稳健，要如实说。
- **必须回答的问题**：10 帧留出集里，**有几帧能稳定地给出球门线与边线**？逐帧列出。如果只有几帧成功，说清成功的帧有什么共同点（例如机位较远、草坪占比大）。
- **算力成本要报**：这些操作用了什么算法、单帧耗时多少。因为最终目标是搬到 Zynq-7020 的 PL（本项目 PL 卷积引擎实测约 15.3 GOPS；器件只有 53 200 LUT6 / 220 DSP / 4.9 Mb BRAM）。如果某个方法（比如全图 Hough）在你实测里单帧耗时远超 ~100 ms，要明确指出它不适合上板，并说明有没有更省的替代（例如只在草坪掩膜内、降采样、只扫特定角度范围）。

## 硬约束（本仓库既有纪律，违反会被门禁挡下）

关键认识是：对投影变换而言，四条线恰好有 8 个自由度，所以只要存在某个单应，任何四条检测到的线都能映射到一个矩形；单条线约束的几何本身没有判别力。真正的约束在于：这个单应还必须能分解为一个刚体相机，且焦距相等、主点位于像心。我要据此重新设计。

这个单应可用，但我犯了一个真实的数学错误：`H = λK[r1 r2 t]` 中 r1、r2 是 H 的**列**，不是行；而且强制 `h33=1` 在数值上会失败（列值达到 76 和 383）。我改用正确的 DLT 零空间来修正，增加一个合成单元测试，并枚举所有边线对，而不是贪心地只挑一对：

草坪包络是一个 **强语义先验**，而之前的检测完全没有用到它：远端边线等于草坪区域的上边界，也就是广告牌带的底边；近端边线等于草坪区域的下边界，也就是跑道边。广告牌边缘只贴一侧，所以无法同时满足这两个条件。

草坪包络是我一直没用上的最强语义信号，也可直接用来**合成**远端边线候选，不必依赖它被 Hough 找到。我先确认线段元组的字段布局：

## 结论一句话
**不可行（端到端）**：判据本身成立且有判别力（负对照全 0、oracle 证明可达、失败模式随 gate 放松单调迁移），但 640×360 转播画质下 12 cm 画线只有 0.24–0.58 px，白线掩膜沿真实边线覆盖率 0.00–0.76 且碎成虚线，两条边线同时 ≥0.55 结构上不可达；HD 对照 0 通过，分辨率也不是解。球门盲检 precision 2/7。需要的条件：近机位/半自动初始化/割草条纹等替代场地先验。

Let me look at the data: MANIFEST.csv, frames, and existing repo structure (results dir, evaluator script).

Let me plan the approach:

1. Read MANIFEST.csv to get frames + timestamps.
2. Look at a few frames to understand camera views.
3. Design white line segmentation: local contrast based (top-hat filter on lightness), plus low saturation, plus green-neighborhood context (line should be surrounded by grass on both sides).
4. Hough lines with angle constraints, then geometric labeling: use vanishing point estimation. On a soccer pitch with a broadcast camera, the two touchlines converge to a vanishing point; goal line is roughly horizontal-ish but also has a vanishing point. Actually the robust approach: estimate grass mask, then within grass mask, find lines. Two dominant vanishing points: one for lines parallel to touchline (touchlines + goal area lines parallel to touchline... wait).

Let me think about pitch geometry properly:
- Pitch rectangle: touchlines (long, along x), goal lines (short, along y).
- Lines parallel to touchlines: touchlines themselves, and... the penalty area lines? No. Penalty area is a rectangle: 16.5m into field from goal line, width 40.32m. So penalty area has two lines parallel to goal line (at 16.5m and the goal line itself) and two lines parallel to touchline (the sides of the penalty area). Center line is parallel to goal line (perpendicular to touchline). Circle is at center. Penalty arc bulges from penalty area toward center.
- So: family A (parallel to touchline, direction along pitch length): touchlines (2), penalty area sides (4, but only 2 visible typically the near ones), goal area sides (4). 
- Family B (perpendicular to touchline): goal lines (2), center line, penalty area front lines (2), goal area front lines (2).

Vanishing points: family A → VP_A; family B → VP_B. These two VPs lie on the horizon line, and ideally the angle between directions... In the image, "90° on the ground plane" means the angle measured in the rectified bird's-eye view, which requires a homography. But a projective invariant: two lines' vanishing points define the horizon; the angle between two ground-plane directions can be computed if we know camera intrinsics... Alternatively: use the vanishing point of the vertical direction? Not available.

Simpler geometric self-consistency that doesn't need intrinsics:
Given the horizon line (line through VP_A and VP_B) and the two VPs, the angle θ between the two ground directions satisfies (from standard result):
cos θ = ... depends on focal length f and the positions of the VPs. Specifically for two vanishing points v1, v2 with directions d1, d2 on the ground plane perpendicular to each other, we have the constraint (v1 - p)·(v2 - p) + f² = 0 where p is principal point (image center) with f focal. Actually the standard: d1 ∝ K^{-1} v1, and d1·d2 = 0 → v1^T K^{-T}K^{-1} v2 = 0. With K = diag(f,f,1) and centered principal point: (x1 x2 + y1 y2)/f² + 1 = 0 → f² = -(x1 x2 + y1 y2).

So: from the two VPs (and assumed principal point at image center), we can solve for f: f² = -(x1x2 + y1y2). If the solution is real and positive and gives plausible f (e.g. f between 0.5W and 4W for a broadcast camera), the geometry is self-consistent and perpendicular. That's a beautiful criterion: "orthogonality residual" = f_est, and we can also compute the actual angle given a nominal f.

Better approach that's directly interpretable and checkable: 
1. Estimate VP_A (intersection of the two touchlines) and VP_B (intersection of goal line with... well we need two lines of family B; we have the goal line and the center line, or penalty area front line).
2. Compute f from orthogonality constraint. Report residual: |f_est - f_nominal| / f_nominal where f_nominal is... unknown. Hmm.

Alternative: use a "reprojection to bird's eye" using a homography derived from 4 points, then check rectangle-ness. But that's circular.

Pragmatic criterion that is honest and fails properly:
- Fit a homography H from detected line set: assume pitch model (standard FIFA: 105×68 m). Using the two touchlines + goal line intersections we get 3 of 4 corners of the pitch rectangle (far touchline ∩ goal line, near touchline ∩ goal line, and ... actually the goal line is one line, touchlines two → 3 intersections; the 4th corner (other goal line) is out of frame typically).
- Alternative: Use vanishing points + horizon, and then verify with a metric rectification: given horizon l_h and one VP pair with known perpendicularity, compute the "image of the absolute conic" ω = K^{-T}K^{-1} assuming square pixels and known principal point. Then rectify and check that the pitch lines become axis-aligned and that the aspect ratio of e.g. the penalty area (40.32 × 16.5 = 2.443) matches. That's a strong test but complicated.

Let me simplify to something defensible and implementable within time:

**Self-consistency residual** (per frame), three sub-residuals:
1. `r_orth`: Let v1 = intersection of the two touchline candidates (near & far), v2 = intersection of goal line with center line (or with the penalty-area front line, whichever pair is available). Assume principal point at image center, square pixels. Orthogonality of ground directions requires (v1-c)·(v2-c) + f² = 0 → f² = -(v1-c)·(v2-c). Require f² > 0 and f in a plausible range. Residual: the "orthogonality error" measured as angle between the two directions at an assumed focal length f0 = 1.2×image width (typical broadcast zoom varies...). Hmm, f unknown so the angle is f-dependent.

Honestly a cleaner and fully defensible route: **use the homography from 4 detected line intersections + known pitch dimensions, then measure the reprojection residual of all detected lines**. That is: assume the pitch model (105×68). From 2 VPs + horizon we get the plane's metric structure up to scale and translation... 

OK here's the clean method (standard "single view metrology"), workable:
- The two VPs v1 (touchline direction) and v2 (goal line direction) plus the horizon give the plane's affine structure. A homography that maps the ground plane to a metric top view: H = H_a * H_p where H_p = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[l1,l2,l3]] (horizon line l = (l1,l2,l3)) and H_a = [[1/|...|]] using v1, v2 as the vanishing points of the two orthogonal directions. Standard result: after affine rectification, the two VPs become directions at infinity: v1' and v2' at infinity. Then apply H_a = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[ ... ]] hmm. The known formula: with v1=(a,b,0) and v2=(c,d,0) at infinity and v1·v2 = ac+bd... metric rectification requires the dual conic. Formula: H = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[l1,l2,l3]] then H_a = [[1/b1, 0,0],[0,1/b2,0],[0,0,1]]... 

This is getting deep. Let me use a much more practical and *verifiable* formulation that a reviewer can audit:

**Practical criterion: two-stage.**
Stage 1 (structure): require detection of ≥1 touchline-family line + ≥1 goal-line-family line, with the two VPs consistent (they must both be finite and the horizon line through them must not be degenerate). Family assignment comes from clustering line directions into 2 dominant directions (that's the data-driven "two vanishing points" clustering, no pitch knowledge needed).
Stage 2 (metric): use the orthogonality-in-image constraint with *unknown* focal length, solve for f² = -(v1-c)·(v2-c). Then residual = how far the implied f is from a plausible camera range, plus a **direct check**: reproject a synthetic pitch model. Concretely:
- With (f, c) known we get K. Then rectified top-view: for a ground point p, the ray K^{-1}p intersects ground plane... but we don't know the camera height/orientation. However, with 2 VPs and 1 known direction... Hmm, we know the horizon, and we know the pitch is 68 m wide between the touchlines. 

Actually we DO have enough: given K, v1, v2, and the *distance between the two touchlines* (68 m) plus their positions in the image, we can build a full homography H mapping the ground plane to a metric top view. The construction:
1. From v1, v2, K: rotation R known up to the sign/scale. Specifically, direction of touchline d1 = K^{-1}v1 normalized; goal line d2 = K^{-1}v2 normalized; both lie in the ground plane; vertical d3 = d1 × d2. Then the plane's normal n = d3. Camera pose: R = [d1, d2, d3]^T maps world → camera (assuming world axes along pitch). Camera height h and translation determined up to scale by the constraint that the two touchlines are 68 m apart... The distance between two parallel lines in the world = 68 m gives the scale.
2. Then H = the map from top-view (X, Y) in meters to image: for ground point (X, Y, 0) with camera at height h: p = K [r1 r2 t] (X,Y,1). t has 3 unknowns, but the plane's normal constraint plus known h... 

Simpler: homography from 4 point correspondences. If we have 4 identified pitch points (e.g., 3 corners where goal line meets the two touchlines = 2 points, plus penalty area intersections, etc.), we can do DLT and then check residuals — but identifying which physical point is which is exactly the hard part.

**Decision: use a pragmatic, auditable residual with explicit assumption.** I'll define:

- Cluster lines into 2 families by angle (weighted by length) → v1, v2 via least squares (each family's lines should concur at a point; the RMS distance from lines to their family VP is the concurrence residual `r_vp` in pixels — this is a genuine geometric consistency test).
- Compute horizon line = v1 × v2.
- Assume principal point c = image center, square pixels. Orthogonality ⟹ f² = -(v1-c)·(v2-c). Report `f_implied_px` and require it in [0.5W, 6W] (a wide range that covers broadcast zoom from wide to tight). If f² < 0 → the two families are not perpendicular under any focal length → hard fail.
- `r_orth`: measured angle between the two ground directions at the implied f is by construction 90°, so instead report **the angle between them as seen in the image** (`ang_img`) and, more useful, the **f sensitivity**: compute the angle the two directions would make if f = f_implied; that's 90 by construction. So instead I'll report the *plausibility*: f_implied in pixels / image width, and the deviation of the implied f from a canonical f0 = 1.0*W... no.

Hmm, let me reframe: the truly meaningful check for "is this a real pitch rectangle" is **the reprojection of the pitch model**. And it's actually easy to do if I'm willing to *assume a camera model* and *fit it*: 

Fit a 6-8 DoF camera (f, pitch/yaw/roll, height, 2 translation) to the detected lines by minimizing point-to-line distances of the projected FIFA pitch model lines. That's a full calibration-by-line-matching, which is the *right* way and yields a residual in pixels. It's maybe 100 lines of code with scipy least_squares. And a true pitch will fit with residual ~1-2 px; an advertising board will not fit at all (it'll converge to a bad local min with large residual, or the initialization from VPs will be far off).

But careful: fitting 8 DoF to only 2-3 lines is underconstrained → will overfit and produce small residuals even for garbage. Need to guard: require ≥3 lines from both families, and check f and height are plausible, and check the fitted goal line's distance... Also the classic degeneracy.

Given time constraints, I'll do a layered approach:
- **Primary residual (used for pass/fail)**: `r_vp` = RMS pixel distance of family lines from their common vanishing point, combined with a *rectification residual*: build homography from VPs assuming f0 = implied f (or a fixed nominal), rectify the image so families are axis-aligned, then measure how straight/parallel/o... 

You know what — there IS a clean, cheap, and honest primary criterion available: **parallelism after rectification by the two VPs**. Specifically:
- Compute H_affine that sends horizon to infinity (standard: H_p = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[l1,l2,l3]]). Apply to the two families: each family's lines should become *parallel* (they all pass through their VP which is now at infinity). Residual: after rectification, the angle spread within each family (max-min angle in degrees). That's a real test of "do these lines actually concur at one point". This is exactly `r_vp` expressed as an angle. Good.
- Then **orthogonality**: in the affine-rectified image, compute the angle between family mean directions. Affine rectification does NOT restore angles (needs metric rectification), so this angle is not 90° in general. To get metric, we need the circular points — which needs either known perpendicularity (circular!) or known aspect ratio, or camera intrinsics. With square pixels + principal point assumption we get f, and then full metric rectification → the angle becomes exactly 90° by construction. So orthogonality cannot be *tested* independently without another constraint... UNLESS we use a known length ratio, e.g., the penalty area is 40.32 × 16.5 m (ratio 2.443), or the pitch is 105 × 68 (1.544). 

So the honest test: **use the pitch aspect ratio 105:68 as the metric constraint.** From the metric-rectified view (using f from orthogonality), the two touchlines must be 68 apart and the goal line length visible... but the far goal line is usually out of frame, so the 105 m length isn't measurable. However, the penalty area depth 16.5 m IS measurable if both the goal line and the penalty-area front line are detected, and 40.32 m width if both penalty-area sides are visible. Their ratio 40.32/16.5 = 2.443 is a scale-free metric test! And the goal area 18.32 × 5.5.

Practical plan I'll actually implement (keep it tractable):

**Detection pipeline:**
1. Read frame 640×360.
2. Grass mask: HSV green range + morphology (reuse green_ratio logic but as mask).
3. White-line candidate mask inside grass: 
   - Convert to Lab, use L channel.
   - Local contrast: top-hat with a long thin structuring element (e.g., 15×15 or oriented 9×9) — or better: `L - opening(L, 9x9)` catches thin bright structures. Also require low saturation (S < 60) and L above local median by margin.
   - Require pixel within grass mask or within a small dilation of grass mask (lines are on grass; ball/goal are on grass too).
   - Remove big blobs: connected components with area > threshold and low elongation → reject (advertising boards, jerseys). Keep components whose elongation is high (fit line / minAreaRect aspect) or thin (area/perimeter).
4. Line detection: `cv2.HoughLinesP` on the cleaned mask (and also standard HoughLines for VP estimation? HoughLinesP gives segments). Filter by min length.
5. Merge collinear segments, cluster by angle → 2 dominant families using a weighted histogram of angles modulo 180 with peak picking (or RANSAC on line normals to find two VPs — better: use the classic "two vanishing points from lines" via RANSAC over pairs of lines: sample 2 lines, hypothesize VP, count inliers).
6. Label families: family with smaller |angle| to horizontal... Hmm. Which family is touchlines vs goal lines? Actually there's a neat projective invariant-free test: for a plane, the two vanishing points V1, V2 of two orthogonal directions, and the horizon line l_h = V1 × V2. The imaged line at infinity. Orthogonality of the directions gives constraint on the image of the absolute conic: V1^T ω V2 = 0. With unknown focal f and principal point at image center (reasonable for broadcast, and we can even fix f to a plausible value or solve), we can test whether the measured angle between the two directions in the "back-projected" plane is ~90°. So condition: x1*x2 + y1*y2 + 1/f² = 0. This determines f purely! f² = -1/(x1x2+y1y2).

**Self-consistency criterion (primary)**: 
1. Compute vanishing points V1 (from near/far touchline family), V2 (from goal-line family) using RANSAC.

Then compute H fully. Residual threshold e.g. RMS < 4 px and >=3 distinct roles supported.

Then self-consistency: project remaining template features (penalty area at x=16.5, goal area x=5.5, center circle, far goal line x=105, penalty spot) and check support in the line mask. And also, cross-check: residual of the 4 lines themselves is zero by construction, so the residual must come from *additional* features, plus from the *reprojected* consistency like:

But wait: a residual of 0 on the 4 chosen lines is trivially satisfied by *any* 4 lines. That's the "n=1" trap. The discriminating power must come from: (i) unassigned/extra detected lines must be explained by the template (i.e., all long lines in the grass region should map to some template line — unexplained long lines = negative evidence); (ii) predicted-but-not-detected template lines; (iii) plausibility of f and of the projected pitch quad (aspect ratio of ground-plane? no, that's fixed); (iv) the mask support along predicted lines that weren't used in the fit.

So the criterion I'll use:

**Residual R** = combined, per frame:
1. `rms_px`: RMS perpendicular distance of *all* detected line pixels (from segments not used in the 4-line fit, classified as pitch-like by being long) to the nearest projected template line. Low = good.
2. `unexplained_frac`: fraction of total detected segment length not within 3 px of any projected template line.
3. `extra_support`: mean mask coverage along projected template lines NOT used in fit (penalty area, goal area, center circle, far goal line). High = good (independent confirmation).
4. `f_plausible`: focal length in plausible range.
5. `quad_ok`: the projected pitch quad covers a sensible area & its far edge is above the near edge etc.

PASS requires: rms_px < 4, unexplained_frac < 0.45, extra_support >= 0.35 on >=2 independent template features, f plausible.

Crucially, the negative control (top half / crowd region) must FAIL. With no grass mask, segments will be ad-board edges etc. The 4-line assignment heuristic (pick 2 long horizontals + 2 long verticals) will produce some H, but then extra_support on penalty-area lines should be ~0 and unexplained_frac high. Good — that gives discriminative power. That's a real experiment; results are what they are.

### Step 1: pitch/grass mask
HSV: green hue. Use relative: G > R + k and G > B + k, plus saturation. Rather than absolute luminance. Also normalize by local mean brightness to handle night/day.

Actually a robust approach: compute green chroma ratio: `g_excess = G - max(R,B)`, plus normalized: `G/(R+G+B)`. Then grass mask = g_excess > threshold & not too dark. Then morphological closing to fill lines (white lines are holes in grass mask). Then pitch region = largest connected component (convex hull) of grass mask — the field. This handles stands (not green), advertising boards (red/blue/white).

### Step 2: white line mask
White = high intensity AND low saturation. But also must be *relative*: local contrast. Use `V` normalized by local blurred V? Actually white lines are brighter than surrounding grass. Compute ratio: `white_score = V / (local_mean_V + eps)` — on grass, lines stand out ~1.3-2x. On ad boards, background is already bright so ratio ~1.0. On white jerseys the ratio is also high — but jerseys are blobs, not thin structures. Then filter by shape (thin elongated) via morphology: opening with line kernels, or check that the blob is thin.

- Remove components with minAreaRect: require (long side >= 25 px) and (long/short >= 4) and fill ratio (area / rect area <= 0.6). That kills jerseys (roughly square blobs).

### Step 3: line detection
HoughLinesP on the white mask, restricted to pitch ROI, with theta resolution 0.5 deg... Standard Hough with `cv2.HoughLines` (rho,theta) gives global lines better for vanishing point analysis. But time cost. On 640×360, HoughLinesP is ~2-5 ms. HoughLines (standard) maybe 5 ms. Fine.

Then merge collinear segments (cluster by (theta, rho)). Keep lines with total support length >= threshold.

With z=1: v1·v2 + f² = 0 → f² = -(v1x v2x + v1y v2y). So given two vanishing points you get f². Legit constraint: f² > 0 and f must be plausible for a broadcast lens (e.g., f in [0.6, 2.5] × image width).

That's a real, falsifiable consistency check! And it also gives you a way to reject wrong line assignments: if the two VPs yield f² <= 0 (i.e., the angle between them is obtuse, meaning they're not perpendicular), reject.

If below is mostly non-grass and above is grass → near touchline. If both sides are grass → it's an interior line (penalty area line, goal area line).

   That's a solid, physical discriminator.

The far goal line: above it is ads/stands (just beyond the goal line is the boundary of the pitch, then ads). But so is the far touchline... no, the far touchline is Family A.

   Honestly: the far goal line usually appears as the line at the top of the grass region bounding the visible pitch, running left-right, near the ad boards. Discriminator: Family-B line, near the far edge of pitch mask, above it is non-grass, below is grass. Also perpendicular to touchlines at plausible scale.

   - r_offset: distance from the far goal line to the far touchline's intersection — they must meet at a corner. Compute intersection point of near/far touchline with far goal line; the corner should be near the pitch polygon boundary and the two corners should be within the grass mask. Report corner distance to grass mask boundary.
   - r_model: fraction of the *expected* pitch structure supported.

Now the algorithm details.

**Grass mask**: 
```
img RGB float. r,g,b
chroma green: g - max(r,b) > 8 (relative, scale-invariant-ish)
also normalized: g/(r+g+b+1) > 0.36
grass = green & (g > 30)   # darkness guard for night
```
Night frames: it's a night match (the frames look like evening). Actually all frames appear to be the same match with floodlights. The task mentions day/overcast/night — I'll compute per-frame median brightness and report; thresholds must be relative.

No — grass is only inside the pitch plus maybe outside the boundary there's also grass in some stadiums. In match_040 there's a red running track outside. In match_004, outside the far touchline is the ad board. Near the bottom there's the track/ads.)

**White mask** (key deliverable 1):
- Convert to float. Compute local mean of V (or of the max channel) via a box blur of size 15 (relative threshold).

- Simpler & more robust for broadcast: use the theta histogram. Simpler practical approach: use the camera model. With f from orthogonality and principal point at center:
- Direction of family A in camera frame: d1 ∝ K⁻¹ v1 (normalized), d2 ∝ K⁻¹ v2. Since orthogonal, d1·d2 ≈ 0 by construction of f.
- Up vector: u = d1 × d2 (normalized). This is the ground-plane normal (if both families lie on the ground plane). 
- Then the ground plane normal n = u. Camera height h unknown. A point on the ground plane projects... Actually to map image to ground plane coordinates we need h. But we can compute ground coordinates up to scale h: for a pixel p, ray r = K⁻¹p; intersect with plane {X: n·X = h} → X = h * r/(n·r). So ground coords (in meters) = h * (x,y) and we can set h=1 → coordinates in units of camera height. Then measure the pitch rectangle in those units: touchline separation / goal-line separation should be 68/105 = 0.6476. THAT is a beautiful, fully over-determined, scale-free geometric test! Because scale h cancels in the ratio.

4. Compute the pitch footprint: the ground-plane quadrilateral formed by the outermost lines of each family. Compute its width/length ratio in ground units. So implementation:
- Cluster lines into families by angle+VP.
- Pick the family with the largest total support/spread as "touchline family" (A). Within it, take the two lines with the largest perpendicular separation (near/far).
- Pick from family B the line that is most "goal-line-like": the one that intersects both A lines and whose intersections lie within image bounds; typically the one with highest support.
- Compute the rectifying homography H from VPs (using f), map directions: for each line, direction vector in ground plane = H applied to its point at infinity. Angle between A-direction and B-direction ⇒ report.
- Residual: (1) angle deviation from 90°; (2) A-VP inlier RMS.

Special case: if only ONE family is found (e.g. only touchlines visible), no 90° test — report fail with reason "single_family".

Code sketch:

```python
import sys
sys.dont_write_bytecode = True
sys.stdout.reconfigure(encoding="utf-8")
import time
from pathlib import Path
import numpy as np, cv2

ROOT = Path(r"D:\zynq-yolo")
CAL = ROOT/"data/samples/calib_real/frames_calib"
TST = ROOT/"data/samples/calib_real/frames_test"
OUT = ROOT/"data/results/viz/pitch"
OUT.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

def grass_mask(bgr):
    hsv = cv2.cvtColor(bgr, cv2.COLOR_BGR2HSV)
    h,s,v = cv2.split(hsv)
    g = ((h>=30)&(h<=100)&(s>=40)&(v>=25)).astype(np.uint8)*255
    g = cv2.morphologyEx(g, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(9,9)))
    n, lab, stats, cent = cv2.connectedComponentsWithStats(g, 8)
    if n<=1: return np.zeros_like(g)
    k = 1+int(np.argmax(stats[1:, cv2.CC_STAT_AREA]))
    m = (lab==k).astype(np.uint8)*255
    m = cv2.morphologyEx(m, cv2.MORPH_CLOSE, cv2.getStructuringElement(cv2.MORPH_ELLIPSE,(15,15)))
    return m
```

wh = (th > k) & (s < 70) & (v > 70)

Key physics: pitch lines are **painted on grass**, so the line pixels are grass texture with white paint — meaning locally the pixel is brighter and less saturated than surrounding grass, and the line is thin (2-4 px at 640 wide) and long. Key design decisions:

1. Grass mask: HSV green + largest CC + close. Robust across day/night since H/S criteria relative. Also could add: the field is the largest connected region.

2. White-line candidate mask: local top-hat (relative) on V, plus low saturation, masked to grass-dilated area. Then filter components by elongation & width.

3. Hough: cv2.HoughLinesP on the line mask, with rho=1, theta=pi/180, threshold ~ 40, minLineLength ~ 40, maxLineGap ~ 8.

4. Merge collinear segments into lines (cluster by (theta, rho)).

5. Classification & geometric self-consistency:
   - Vanishing point of the two touchlines: the two touchlines (near/far) are parallel in 3-D, so in image they intersect at a vanishing point V1.
   - The goal lines (two) are parallel in 3-D and perpendicular to touchlines in the ground plane. Their vanishing point V2 must satisfy: the angle between the two vanishing directions... Hmm, in perspective, for two perpendicular directions on the ground plane, with camera intrinsic K and assuming zero skew/known focal length, we have v1^T ω v2 = 0 where ω = K^-T K^-1. That's the standard constraint. With unknown f (focal length in px) and principal point at center, we can derive f from orthogonality: for v1=(x1,y1,1), v2=(x2,y2,1), ω = diag(1,1,1/f²) roughly (with principal point at image center): x1x2 + y1y2 + 1/f² = 0 → f² = -1/(x1x2+y1y2) when x1x2+y1y2 < 0.

   That gives a great self-consistency criterion: two touchlines + two goal lines → estimate f from orthogonality, and check that the implied f is in a plausible range (e.g. 300-3000 px for a 640x360 broadcast crop, actual broadcast lens ~ f=700-1500 px equivalent). That is a strong, falsifiable criterion: random lines will give negative or absurd f.

   That's much better than "angle 90° in image" (which is wrong in perspective). The task says "边线与球门线在近地平面上的夹角应接近 90°" — in the ground plane, yes = 90°. In the image, we can validate via the vanishing-point orthogonality test. Also we can do a full homography rectification and check the pitch is a rectangle with plausible dimensions (105x68 ratio 1.54).

   Actually even better: fit a homography from the 4 detected lines... but we only have lines, not corners. However two touchlines + two goal lines give 4 lines → their 4 intersections give the 4 corners of the pitch. Then homography H maps those 4 points to a 105x68 rectangle. Then check: the center circle (radius 9.15 m) maps to a circle of radius 9.15 m in the rectified plane. That's a beautiful self-consistency check! And it validates arcs too.

   That's the killer criterion: after rectification using the 4 pitch lines, the center circle's ellipse must map to a circle of radius ≈9.15 m (and penalty area lines 16.5m, goal 7.32m). But the rectified check requires the arcs be detected. Maybe too ambitious; can include if feasible.

   - Residual = |log(f/f_ref)| plus reprojection of the 4 corners to a rectangle: compute homography from the 4 corners → 105x68 rectangle, then measure residual of the 4 lines fitting the rectified rectangle edges + aspect? So plan for the consistency residual per frame:
1. Group detected long lines into 4 pitch boundary lines: two "touchline" candidates (long, roughly converging) and two "goal line" candidates.
2. Compute VP1 (touchlines intersection), VP2 (goal lines intersection).
3. f from orthogonality; if impossible → fail with reason.
4. Corners = intersections of each touchline with each goal line (4 points).
5. H: rectifies corners to (0,0),(105,0),(105,68),(0,68).
6. Residual = check that the detected "penalty area line" (if any) maps to x=16.5 (or 105-16.5) within tolerance, and the center line maps to x=52.5.

Let me reconsider. What about a purely projective, camera-independent test that is still strong:

Given the 4 "pitch frame" lines aren't available, use this instead: **the parallel-line test.** In a real pitch image, lines come in two parallel families (touchline family, goal-line family). Each family's members must share a common vanishing point. That's a projective invariant requiring NO camera calibration. Test:
- Take all detected long line segments. Cluster by vanishing point (Hough in dual space / RANSAC for VP).
- A frame "passes" if we find two VPs V1, V2, each supported by ≥2 lines, and:
  * V1 and V2 are *different and well-separated* (so the two families aren't the same).
  * The implied focal length from orthogonality is in a plausible range (this uses the assumption that the two families are perpendicular in 3-D, which is TRUE for pitch touchlines vs goal lines/penalty-area lines/center line — all pitch lines are either parallel or perpendicular to the touchline!). This is the key: **every pitch line is either parallel or perpendicular to the touchline.** So all detected lines belong to exactly 2 direction families, and those two families are perpendicular in the ground plane. That's a very strong, pitch-specific constraint.
  * The third line family (ad boards, stand edges, etc.) is generally NOT parallel/perpendicular to the pitch, so it will form its own VP not satisfying orthogonality with the pitch families.

So the criterion becomes: **do the detected lines condense into exactly two perpendicular direction families, with a physically plausible focal length?** That is falsifiable, camera-model-based (single unknown f), and doesn't require both goal lines to be visible. Penalty area lines, center line, goal lines are all in the "goal-line family" (perpendicular to touchline); the touchlines and the 6-yard box sides are in the touchline family. 

And the negative control (top half of image with stands/ad boards) — the ad boards are typically parallel to the touchline (they run along the pitch!) so they'd form a family parallel to the touchline. In the top crop you might get ad board lines (one family) plus stand edges (another family, but not perpendicular). So it should fail the orthogonal-f test. That's honest and reportable.

Decision: keep it tractable and honest. The consistency test I'll implement:

**Per frame scoring with a two-family (Manhattan-world) fit:**

Also important: we must produce geometric self-consistency via homography. Let me think about the actual math now, since that's the crux.

Model: standard soccer pitch, length L=105 m (goal to goal), width W=68 m. Coordinates: x along length 0..105, y along width 0..68. Camera (broadcast) is usually on the side at height h, looking at the pitch. Unknown: homography H (8 dof) from pitch plane to image.

Detected primitives: a set of long straight segments with angles. In a broadcase wide view, the visible lines are:
- near touchline (y=0)
- far touchline (y=68)
- goal line (x=0 or x=105)
- halfway line (x=52.5)
- penalty area lines (x=16.5 and x=88.5) etc.

We don't know which detected segment is which. Full assignment search is expensive. But we can do a hypothesis-driven approach:

Given the camera is elevated and roughly on the touchline side, a plausible approach:
H1: The two touchlines are nearly horizontal in image (they converge at a vanishing point). In image, near touchline is lower, far touchline is upper. Both should be detected as long lines.
H2: goal line is a nearly-vertical line (converging too).

Then compute vanishing points; but with only 2 lines from one direction and 1 from another, the VP of the goal-line direction is determined only if we have ≥2 parallel lines in that direction (e.g., goal line + halfway line + penalty area lines).

Approach to self-consistency that is measurable and falsifiable:
Given 2 "touchline" candidates (nearly parallel in 3D, so in image they intersect at VP_y) and 2 "goal-direction" lines (goal line and halfway line, intersect at VP_x), plus the 90° constraint between the two families, we can solve the camera focal length f and rotation R analytically (classic single-view calibration from two vanishing points of orthogonal directions). If both VPs exist and the implied f is plausible (f in [0.6W_img, 3W_img]... in pixels maybe 400..2000 for 640 wide with typical broadcast lens ~ 30-60° FOV → f ≈ 640/(2 tan(30°)) ≈ 554), then consistent.

That's a nice geometric self-consistency test: orthogonal vanishing points ⇒ f² = -(u1-uc)(u2-uc) - (v1-vc)(v2-vc) (with principal point at image center). If the dot-product of the two vanishing directions in camera coords gives 90°, then f is real. Actually the formula: for VP1=(u1,v1), VP2=(u2,v2), with principal point (u0,v0), the condition that the two 3D directions are orthogonal is:
(u1-u0)(u2-u0)/f² + (v1-v0)(v2-v0)/f² + 1 = 0
⇒ f² = -[(u1-u0)(u2-u0) + (v1-v0)(v2-v0)]

So f² must be positive and yield plausible f. That's a strong, falsifiable geometric criterion. Good — it's exactly "边线与球门线在近地平面上的夹角应接近 90°" measured through the camera model.

Even better, we can then compute full homography: from 2 VPs + their line positions + the known pitch dims (assuming line identity: e.g., goal line x=0, halfway x=52.5, near touchline y=0, far y=68), we can build homography from 4 correspondences: the 4 intersection points of those lines (corners of the region). But intersections of nearly-parallel lines are far away numerically unstable. Alternative: use the metric rectification from the two VPs and known distances to derive H via a standard "two VP + one known length" method. That's more robust: 
- Set camera coords, rotate so that VP directions known.

Alternative pragmatic approach for self-consistency that's still real geometry and much simpler:
Use the two vanishing points to define the plane's rectification up to an affine (metric) transform. Formula: A homography that rectifies plane given VP1, VP2 and f:
H = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[ (u1-u0)/f² ... ]] hmm.

Standard: The line at infinity of the plane is l∞ = (v1 × v2) — the vanishing line. Metric rectification needs the circular points; with two orthogonal VPs: the circular points constraint gives the image of the absolute conic ω = diag(1,1,f²)... something. Then H_rect = H_p where H_p is the projective transform mapping l∞ to (0,0,1) and satisfying the metric constraints. This is doable: 
Given ω (IAC) = K^-T K^-1 with K = diag(f,f,1), then the rectifying homography that maps image plane to metric plane is H = H_a * H_p where H_p maps l∞ to infinity. Actually a standard result: for a plane with vanishing line l=(l1,l2,l3), the affine rectification is
H_p = [[1,0,0],[0,1,0],[l1,l2,l3]]
and then metric rectification S (the dual conic of circular points) etc. Getting exact is fiddly, but there is a clean way:

Direct linear transform on lines (DLT with line correspondences) — we can estimate H minimizing algebraic error of mapped lines. That's elegant: for each matched pair (pitch line in 3D plane, image line), the constraint is that H^-T l_img is parallel to the 3D line's... Actually simpler: a 3D plane point X on the pitch line (a,b) maps to image x = H X. For the image line l, constraint l^T H X = 0 for all X on the pitch line ⇒ l^T H [line in pitch coords] = 0 → 2 linear constraints on H entries. With ≥4 line-correspondences (each giving 2 constraints, H has 8 dof up to scale) we can solve linearly.

That's a nice robust linear method: given ≥4 assigned pitch lines with image lines, solve H by SVD. Then residual = mean distance of image line pixels to the projected pitch line. And we can check: reprojection error, plus the implied camera height/position plausibility, plus 90° check implicitly.

Practical approach: classify segments into two orientation families by clustering angles modulo 180 (there should be two dominant directions ~orthogonal in the rectified sense). Then assign: family A (more horizontal) = touchline direction (i.e., lines parallel to the touchline are: touchlines, half line? NO — half line is perpendicular to touchlines).

So family A (horizontal-ish) = {near touchline, far touchline}. Family B (vertical-ish) = {goal line, halfway line, penalty box lines...}.

Given ≥2 lines in family A and ≥2 in family B we can compute two vanishing points, then f, then check plausibility + build a full homography using known pitch dims with an assignment of which B-line is which.

The minimal robust test: 
1. take best pair from A (should be touchlines), best pair from B.
2. compute VP_A, VP_B.
3. f² = -[(uA-u0)(uB-u0)+(vA-v0)(vB-v0)]. Require f² > 0 and f in [300, 3000] px (for 640-wide, plausible focal in px).
4. Additionally require the implied horizon line (joining VP_A and VP_B) to be above the pitch region and the camera height plausible.

Hmm. Let me simplify by using the VP/f approach, which is much cheaper and analytically clean:

Step 1: segments → 2 orientation clusters (family A ~ horizontal, family B ~ vertical) using angle histogram / k-means on angle mod 180.
Step 2: compute VP for each family via least-squares intersection of lines (all lines in the family weighted by length). This uses ALL lines and is robust — averaging over many parallel lines gives a very stable VP.
Step 3: f² test.
Step 4: assign touchlines: among family A lines, pick the two longest that are "extreme" (lowest and highest y at image center x) → near/far. But actually the far touchline often is not detected... Hmm, in these frames the far touchline is where ad boards start, often visible as a white line. In match_004 there's a clear white line near the top of the grass. Yes, typically both touchlines visible in wide shots.
Step 5: Using f and VPs, compute R. Then fit camera position with the 2 touchline constraints (y=0, y=68) using least squares on line-to-image-line distances.
Step 6: For each family B line, back-project to pitch x; snap to nearest canonical x in {0, 16.5, 52.5, 88.5, 105}; residual = |x - nearest| in meters. Report.
Step 7: Also project the full pitch model (all canonical lines + center circle + penalty arcs + goals) into the image, and measure white-mask support along those polylines (fraction of sampled points with mask within 2 px). This gives "model coverage" — independent verification.
Step 8: pass if f plausible AND residual < 2.5 m for at least one B line AND model coverage ≥ some threshold.

This is very defensible: the *primary criterion* is the geometric self-consistency (orthogonality via VP + f, and x-snapping residual), plus model-explains-mask coverage. And I report per-frame numbers.

Implementation of homography from lines:
Given pitch model lines (in metres, plane z=0) as (a,b,c) with a*x+b*y+c=0, and image lines (u,v,w) with u*px+v*py+w=0 in pixels. For a 3D line with direction d and a point p on it, param X(t) = (p + t d, 0, 1). Image point = H X. Constraint: l·(H X) = 0 for all t ⇒ two equations: l^T H (p,1) = 0 and l^T H (d,0) = 0.

Let h = vec(H) (row-major 9). Then l^T H X = sum_i l_i * (H X)_i = X^T H^T l = (X ⊗ l)^T h... Let's derive: (H X)_i = sum_j H_ij X_j. l^T H X = sum_i l_i sum_j H_ij X_j = sum_ij l_i X_j H_ij = (l ⊗ X)^T vec_rowmajor(H). Yes with vec_rowmajor H = [H00,H01,H02,H10,...].

So each (X, l) pair gives one row: l_i * X_j arranged. Two X per line (p and d) give 2 rows. 4 lines → 8 rows → solve via SVD null vector. Good, normalize pitch coords to metres/100 to condition.

So the algorithm:
1. segments → families A/B (weighted by length).
2. VP_A = least-squares intersection of A lines; VP_B likewise. Need ≥2 each.
3. f² = -[(uA-u0)(uB-u0) + (vA-v0)(vB-v0)] with principal point at image center. Require f² > 0 → f. Plausibility 250..4000 px.
   Note: this uses the orthogonality assumption; f is the "implied focal". If families are not orthogonal in 3D, f is bogus. Report f and the "angle residual at f=nominal" too.
4. R from VPs and f: direction vectors d_i = K^-1 (u_i,v_i,1) normalized. These are the 3D directions of the pitch x and y axes in camera frame. Set r1 = d_A (x-direction), r2 = d_B (y-direction), r3 = r1 × r2. But sign ambiguity: choose signs so that the camera looks down at the pitch, i.e., pitch normal (0,0,1) maps to a vector pointing up in camera coords with positive ... We'll fix signs by requiring the resulting H to have positive scale and the projected pitch polygon to be convex/sane. Also there's a choice of which family is x vs y (2 options) and sign flips (4) → test a few.
5. For each candidate: near/far touchline assignment (which A segment is y=0 vs 68); solve t via linear LS for a grid of xB; pick best.
6. Score: model coverage on white mask + residual.
7. Pick best hypothesis overall; report.

Careful with the linear-in-t formulation:
H = K [r1 r2 t] where K = [[f,0,cx],[0,f,cy],[0,0,1]].
X = (X,Y,1) → x_img = K (r1 X + r2 Y + t) = K (r1 X + r2 Y) + K t.
Let a = K r1 (3-vector), b = K r2, c = K t. So x_img ∝ a X + b Y + c (homogeneous, up to scale — careful: this isn't a projective map with arbitrary scale; K[r1 r2 t] is a valid H as long as the scale is fixed. Since r1,r2 are unit and orthogonal, the scale is determined; t determines the scale of c. Since the homography can be scaled arbitrarily, we lose nothing.)

Constraint: l^T (aX + bY + c) = 0 for 2 points on the pitch line. So l^T c = -(l^T a X + l^T b Y). Linear in c (3 unknowns). 

So with c known, t = K^-1 c. Then the camera center C = -R^T t. Check: height z must be positive (camera above pitch) and reasonable (5..60 m).

Number of equations: each detected line used gives 2 (two points). With 2 touchlines + 1 B line = 6 equations for 3 unknowns → LS. 

The two points on a pitch line: for touchline y=0: (0,0) and (105,0). For B line at x=xB: (xB,0) and (xB,68).

    add("touchline_near", [(0,0),(L,0)])
    add("touchline_far", [(0,W),(L,W)])
    add("goal_line_left", [(0,0),(0,W)])
    add("goal_line_right", [(L,0),(L,W)])
    add("halfway", [(L/2,0),(L/2,W)])
    for sx in (0,L):
        s = 1 if sx==0 else -1
        # penalty area front
        x = sx + s*16.5
        y0,y1 = 34-20.16, 34+20.16
        add(f"pen_front_{sx}", [(x,y0),(x,y1)])
        add(f"pen_side_near_{sx}", [(sx,y0),(x,y0)])
        add(f"pen_side_far_{sx}", [(sx,y1),(x,y1)])
        xg = sx + s*5.5
        gy0,gy1 = 34-9.16, 34+9.16
        add(f"goalarea_front_{sx}", [(xg,gy0),(xg,gy1)])
        add(f"goalarea_near_{sx}", [(sx,gy0),(xg,gy0)])
        add(f"goalarea_far_{sx}", [(sx,gy1),(xg,gy1)])
    circle center (52.5,34) r 9.15
    penalty arcs center (11,34) r 9.15, portion x>16.5 ; and (94,34) x<88.5
    corner arcs
    goal (3D) posts...
```

Then H = K @ [r1 r2 t] where t = K^{-1} c... careful: c = K t so t = K^{-1} c. And H = K [r1 r2 t] means x = K(r1 X + r2 Y + t). In homogeneous coords the last row of [r1 r2 t] third column is t[2]... and K's third row is [0,0,1] so x_h = f*(...)+cx*(...)+... yes standard.

But precise: x_h = K (r1 X + r2 Y + t). We solve l·(K(r1 X + r2 Y + t)) = 0 where l is a pixel-space line. But l·x_h = 0 is only valid for the normalized pixel coords (x_h = (u,v,1)*w). Since H produces x_h up to scale, and l·x_h=0 is scale-invariant, fine as long as we use consistent line coords: line in homogeneous pixel coords is (a,b,c) with a u + b v + c = 0, and l·x_h = a x_h0 + b x_h1 + c x_h2 = w(a u + b v + c). Key functions:
- `_resample_image(H, pts_m, w, h, nmin=10, nmax=80, step_px=6)` → (N,2) pixel points + owner ids. For each line: project its polyline vertices; accumulate pixel arc length; sample at step_px.

I'll implement `score_model(H, dist, model, w, h)` where model is list of (name, cls, pts_m):
```
tot_hit = 0; tot_n = 0; per = {}
for name, cls, pts in model:
    uv = project(H, pts)
    # resample along pixel path
    seglen = np.linalg.norm(np.diff(uv,axis=0),axis=1)
    if not finite -> skip
    cum = concatenate([[0], cumsum(seglen)])
    L = cum[-1]
    if L < 6: skip (too short to judge)
    n = clip(int(L/5), 4, 90)
    t = linspace(0, L, n)
    sample = interp along path (x and y separately using cum as param)
    mask in-bounds
    d = dist[...]
    hit = d<=2.5
```
This is ~15 lines × ~30 samples = 450 lookups. Fine.

Per-line coverage needs n≥ some min; if the line is barely visible (L<10 px) → mark as not-visible (None) rather than 0, so it doesn't drag coverage down. But then a frame where the halfway line is 8 px would be excluded — correct behaviour, honestly reported as "not visible".

            ...
```

The standard result: with yaw=roll=0, the image of the ground plane satisfies
  u = cx + f·X / D(Y),  v = cy + f·(Y cosτ - h sinτ)/D(Y)... where D = Y sinτ + h cosτ.
So v depends only on Y and u depends on X and Y. And constant-Y lines (length direction) map to horizontal image lines.

So the degenerate-but-common broadcast case: **touchlines horizontal, goal lines vertical**. Then:

**Simpler decision that I'm confident in:** Use the *general* VP method but estimate VPs **robustly and jointly** via a global optimization: 
1. Cluster segments into 2 families by angle with a wide tolerance and by requiring the two clusters' mean directions differ by ≥25°.
2. For each family, compute VP as the least-squares point minimizing Σ (distance from VP to line_i)² / (something) — but for near-parallel lines this is unstable. Use the *normalized* form: minimize Σ w_i * (n_i · vp + c_i)² / |vp|²-ish. With lines in homogeneous form (a_i,b_i,c_i) normalized so a²+b²=1, minimize Σ (a_i x + b_i y + c_i)² over the *unit* vp vector... The standard: the VP is the null vector of A (stacked line coefficients). Solve via SVD, take the smallest singular vector. If the smallest singular value is not much smaller than the next → degenerate (parallel bundle) → VP at infinity, detected honestly. That's clean and robust! Total least squares on homogeneous line coefficients.

3. Orthogonality test: f² = -[(ua-cx)(ub-cx)+(va-cy)(vb-cy)] with VPs possibly at infinity → if |vp| is huge, the term blows up. For VP at infinity in direction d=(dx,dy) normalized, the orthogonality condition becomes... Let me use the 3D direction formulation instead: dirs from VP via K⁻¹·[u,v,1] normalized. If VP at infinity along direction (dx,dy) with |(dx,dy)|=1, then K⁻¹[d,0] = (dx/f, dy/f, 0) normalized = (dx,dy,0). So the 3D direction is horizontal in camera frame. Two such directions are orthogonal iff dx1·dx2+dy1·dy2=0.

General robust orthogonality: given two homogeneous image lines' VPs (as directions), compute 3D dirs d1 = K⁻¹ v1, d2 = K⁻¹ v2 with unknown f. Orthogonality: d1·d2 = 0 → (v1x v2x + v1y v2y)/f² + v1z v2z = 0. If both VPs are at infinity, v1z=v2z=0 → requires v1x v2x + v1y v2y = 0 → i.e., the two families' image directions are perpendicular. That's a *usable, non-circular* test even without f! And in that case f is unconstrained by them (which is the degenerate broadcast case), and we must get f elsewhere or accept it.

Chunk 1 new content:

```python
def _seg_ang(a, b):
    """两条线段方向的锐角差（度）。"""
    d = abs(((a[5] - b[5] + 90.0) % 180.0) - 90.0)
    return float(d)


def _parallel_bundle(segs):
    """一族 3D 平行线在像面上的公共消失点（齐次向量）。

    用归一化直线系数堆成的矩阵 A 的最小奇异向量（总体最小二乘），
    并返回条件数：若 s1/s0 不接近 0，说明这族线**并不共点**，
    即它们不是「3D 平行」，消失点也就没有意义 —— 必须如实报告。
    """
    A = np.array([seg_line(s) for s in segs if seg_line(s) is not None])
    if len(A) < 2:
        return None, 9.9
    A = A / np.linalg.norm(A, axis=1, keepdims=True)
    _u, sv, vt = np.linalg.svd(A, full_matrices=False)
    cond = float(sv[-1] / max(sv[0], 1e-9))
    return vt[-1], cond
```

Then the plane homography fit from lines:

```python
def _dlt_rows(l, X, Y):
    a, b, c = l
    return np.array([a*X, a*Y, a, b*X, b*Y, b, c*X, c*Y]), -c

def fit_h_ground(corrs):
    """由「球场地面直线 <-> 图像直线」对应求 H（8 未知量，h33=1）。

    corrs: [(image_line, [(X,Y), (X,Y)]), ...]，每条对应给 2 行。
    注意 4 条线刚好 8 行 = 恰好定解；>4 条时用最小二乘。
    """
    rows, rhs = [], []
    for l, pts in corrs:
        for (X, Y) in pts:
            r, k = _dlt_rows(l, X, Y)
            rows.append(r); rhs.append(k)
    if len(rows) < 8:
        return None
    h, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(np.asarray(rows), np.asarray(rhs), rcond=None)
    return np.append(h, 1.0).reshape(3, 3)
```

Zhang:

```python
def focal_from_h(H, cx, cy):
    """由 H = K[r1 r2 t] 的两条正交约束反解 f²，返回 (f_a, f_b, 相对差)。

    e1: h1'^T ω h2' = 0      => f² = -(h11 h21 + h12 h22)/(h13 h23)
    e2: h1'^T ω h1' = h2'... => f² = (h11²+h12²-h21²-h22²)/(h23²-h13²)
    两式给两个**独立**的 f 估计。四个检测到的直线在投影意义下总能被某个
    单应解释成矩形 —— 单应本身没有判别力；判别力来自它还必须同时是
    「等焦距、主点在像心的刚体相机」。所以两式的**相对差**就是残差。
    """
    L = centered_h(H, cx, cy)  # 把图像坐标平移到主点
    h11,h12,h13 = L[0]; h21,h22,h23 = L[1]; h31,h32,_ = L[2]
    d1 = h13*h23
    d2 = h23*h23 - h13*h13
    fa = (-(h11*h21 + h12*h22)/d1)**0.5 if abs(d1) > 1e-12 and -(h11*h21+h12*h22)/d1 > 0 else None
    fb = ((h11**2+h12**2-h21**2-h22**2)/d2)**0.5 if abs(d2) > 1e-12 and (h11**2+h12**2-h21**2-h22**2)/d2 > 0 else None
    ...
```

```
def _vp_angle_resid(vp, segs):
...
def dirs_from_vps(vpa, vpb, f, w, h):
    """两个正交消失点 + 焦距 -> 两条 3D 方向（相机系，单位向量）。"""
    cx, cy = w / 2.0, h / 2.0
    K_inv = np.array([[1.0 / f, 0, -cx / f], [0, 1.0 / f, -cy / f], [0, 0, 1.0]])
    d1 = K_inv @ np.array([vpa[0], vpa[1], 1.0])
    d2 = K_inv @ np.array([vpb[0], vpb[1], 1.0])
    return d1 / np.linalg.norm(d1), d2 / np.linalg.norm(d2)
```

The problem: `fit_h_ground` produces H with wildly wrong scale — h33 normalized to 1 but the values are enormous / arbitrary. The fundamental issue: 4 lines give exactly 8 constraints, and h33=1 normalization is arbitrary/ill-conditioned. When the true h33 is near zero, setting h33=1 destroys accuracy.

Better approach: solve for H with all 9 entries up to scale (DLT on the line constraints), then normalize by λ so that the rotation constraint holds. That's the standard Hartley normalization: use SVD to find the null vector, then determine λ (overall scale) such that the decomposition yields unit r1,r2.

But note: 4 lines exactly = 8 constraints = null space dimension 2 for a 3x3 H (9 unknowns) minus scale = 8 dof. So 4 lines exactly determines H up to scale. With DLT, we get a 1-D null space (if rank 8). Good.

Then λ: H = λ K [r1 r2 t]. We need |K^-1 H[:,0]| = |K^-1 H[:,1]| = 1/λ. That gives λ. But f is unknown too. So unknowns: f, λ, and H (8 dof). Hmm, the standard approach: from H = λK[r1 r2 t], we have H' = T H = λ K'[r1 r2 t] where K'=diag(f,f,1). Set B = K'^-T K'^-1 = diag(1/f²,1/f²,1). Constraints:
- h1'^T B h2' = 0
- h1'^T B h1' = h2'^T B h2'

Each gives a linear equation in (1/f², 1) — i.e. in f². That's 2 equations in 1 unknown f², which is overdetermined → use least squares, and the residual measures orthogonality violation. That's much better than computing f from each independently and comparing (which needs both to be positive).

So with a = H[:,0], b = H[:,1], writing B = diag(w, w, 1) with w = 1/f²:
- w(a1 b1 + a2 b2) + a3 b3 = 0 → w = -a3b3/(a1b1+a2b2)
- w(a1²+a2²-b1²-b2²) + (a3²-b3²) = 0 → w = -(a3²-b3²)/(a1²+a2²-b1²-b2²)

Two estimates of w=1/f²: w_a and w_b. Both should be equal and positive. Residual = |w_a - w_b| / mean → that's the self-consistency. And f = 1/sqrt(w).

Let me implement:

```python
def fit_h_ground(corrs):
    rows = []
    for l, pts in corrs:
        if l is None: continue
        a,b,c = l
        for (X,Y) in pts:
            # l^T H [X,Y,1]^T = 0
            # [aX, aY, a, bX, bY, b, cX, cY, c] . h = 0  where h row-major
            rows.append([a*X, a*Y, a, b*X, b*Y, b, c*X, c*Y, c])
    if len(rows) < 8: return None
    A = np.asarray(rows, float)
    # normalize rows to unit norm for conditioning
    A /= np.maximum(np.linalg.norm(A, axis=1, keepdims=True), 1e-12)
    if np.linalg.matrix_rank(A, tol=1e-8) < 8: return None
    _u,_s,vt = np.linalg.svd(A)
    h = vt[-1]
    H = h.reshape(3,3)
    H /= np.linalg.norm(H)
    return H
```

Careful with sign: sign is arbitrary, and the w formula is scale-invariant including sign (ratio of products? w = -a3b3/(a1b1+a2b2): both numerator and denominator are quadratic in H entries, so sign flips cancel). And w = -(a3²-b3²)/(...): also quadratic. Good.

Then focal:

```python
def focal_from_h(H, cx, cy):
    T = ...
    L = T @ H
    a = L[:,0]; b = L[:,1]
    num1 = -a[2]*b[2]; den1 = a[0]*b[0]+a[1]*b[1]
    num2 = -(a[2]**2 - b[2]**2); den2 = a[0]**2+a[1]**2-b[0]**2-b[1]**2
    if abs(den1)<1e-12 or abs(den2)<1e-12: return None,None,None
    w1 = num1/den1; w2 = num2/den2
    if w1<=0 or w2<=0: return None (but record)
    f1 = 1/sqrt(w1); f2=1/sqrt(w2)
    rel = abs(w1-w2)/ (0.5*(w1+w2))   # relative difference of 1/f^2
```

Given plane point X (in pitch coords) maps to image point x = H X. A line in pitch coords L (pitch line) maps to image line l = H^-T L. Conversely L = H^T l.

Pitch line L = (A,B,C) defined by AX+BY+C=0. Image line l = (a,b,c). Then L = H^T l means (A,B,C) = H^T (a,b,c), i.e. L_j = sum_i H_ij l_i. So:
A = H_00 a + H_10 b + H_20 c
B = H_01 a + H_11 b + H_21 c
C = H_02 a + H_12 b + H_22 c

Each is linear in the entries of H. Good. But wait — we also need to handle the scale ambiguity between L and l. Since we normalized L? Hmm, we need L normalized consistently. So cross product: (H^T l) × L = 0, giving 2 independent equations per correspondence. That's the correct approach — it avoids needing L normalized.

Let m = H^T l (a 3-vector, linear in the 9 entries). Cross with known L = (A,B,C):
m × L = 0 →

  m0*B - m1*A = 0
(only 2 independent).

So for each k, m_k = sum over i of h[3i+k]*l[i]. That's a linear functional on h with coefficients: at index 3i+k, coefficient l[i].

row1: C*m1 - B*m2 = 0 → coeffs: C*(m1 coeffs) - B*(m2 coeffs)
row2: A*m2 - C*m0 = 0

```python
def _vec_m(l, k):
    # coeffs for m_k = sum_i H[i,k] l[i], in row-major h
    c = np.zeros(9)
    for i in range(3):
        c[3*i + k] = l[i]
    return c
```

Equality of norms: L00²/f² + L10²/f² + L20² = L01²/f² + L11²/f² + L21²

Practical approach: hypothesis = (which detected segments are touchline A/B, which are transverse line at pitch x=xk). Then fit H from 4 lines. Then measure:

2. camera height plausibility (from H decomposition)
3. **holdout structures**: all pitch lines/arcs NOT used in the fit, projected through H, must land on white pixels. This includes halfway (52.5) unless used, both goal lines, penalty box, goal area, centre circle.

But there's a deeper issue: with a *wrong* assignment, the fitted H maps the 4 lines to the assumed rectangle, and the holdout structures then land in wrong places → low coverage. So the pass criterion is essentially "holdout coverage high AND focal consistent AND height plausible". That's honest and can fail.

However: the greedy `pick_touchlines` picks one pair with a grass prior; then `pick_transverse` picks candidates. The hypothesis search then tries pairs of transverse lines from the candidates with x-pairs from B_CANON. But if the correct transverse line is not among the top `max_b` candidates, all hypotheses fail. Better: keep max_b larger (e.g. 10) and rely on scoring.

Simplest defensible design: define
  rel_orth = |angle(r1,r2) - 90°| / 90°  computed with f hat
  rel_iso  = |‖r1‖-‖r2‖| / 0.5(‖r1‖+‖r2‖)
and f_hat solved by least squares over the two linear-in-1/f² equations. Since both e1 and e2 are linear in u = 1/f², I can stack them and solve by lstsq → always a solution (u could be ≤0 → reject). Then rel = residual of that 2-equation system, normalized. That's clean and always defined when the system is non-singular.

Equations:
 e1: (c0x c1x + c0y c1y) u + c0z c1z = 0
 e2: (c0x²+c0y²-c1x²-c1y²) u + (c0z² - c1z²) = 0
Stack A u = -b, solve u by lstsq; u = 1/f². rel = ||A u + b|| / ||b|| (relative residual). This is a proper 2-equation least-squares orthogonality residual, always defined, and directly comparable across frames.

H = [[h11,h12,h13],[h21,h22,h23],[h31,h32,h33]]. c1 = T@H column 1 = (h12, h22, h32). Then L is normalised by its Frobenius norm. c1[2] = h32/norm. h32 = 0 exactly means the homography has zero Y-dependence in the third row. That means... the ground plane's Y coordinate doesn't affect the projective denominator. That's the signature of a homography where the ground plane maps with rows... Actually if h32=0, the line at infinity of the ground plane maps to a line whose... hmm.

Given H row 3 = [-8e-6, 0, 3.724e-3], denominator = -8e-6*X + 3.724e-3. So the denominator depends only on X, not Y. That means all lines of constant X (i.e. the transverse lines / goal lines) project to *converging* pencil... no wait: for constant X, denominator constant, so u,v are affine in Y → parallel lines in the image. And the "horizon" is where -8e-6 X + 3.724e-3 = 0 → X = 465 m, way off. So the horizon is at infinity effectively → camera looking straight down? No...

If h32=0, the vanishing point of the Y direction (touchline direction) is at H@(0,1,0) = (h12,h22,0) — a point at infinity in the image, meaning touchlines are parallel in the image. And the vanishing point of X direction = (h11,h21,h31). Since the frame is 640x360 and the fit put asg=0/52.5 for two lines... 

This is a degenerate configuration: the fit found touchlines that are exactly parallel in the image (no convergence), which is only true for a camera whose optical axis is parallel to the ground plane (infinite focal / orthographic). So the machinery is right. The problem is purely *detection/grouping recall* on 640×360 broadcast frames. Rather than trying to brute-force 2520 hypotheses, I should go back to a much cheaper, better-conditioned approach:

**Vanishing-point-based approach (standard, and cheap):**
1. Get segments.
2. RANSAC for vanishing points on the segment direction lines. Two dominant VPs (one for each orthogonal direction).
3. Check VPs are orthogonal: for VPs v1,v2 with principal point c: (v1-c)·(v2-c) + f² = 0 → gives f. That's the *self-consistency residual* with only 2 VPs, no need for 4-line homography and no combinatorial explosion.
4. Then use the VP pair + a single scale to build the homography from 2 known points... but we don't know correspondence. Hmm.

Actually with 2 orthogonal VPs and f, plus one line's distance ratio we can construct H up to translation. Hmm, still need correspondence.

But for the *judgment* (main criterion) we don't need the full pitch model. The task's criterion is: two touchlines + goal line → vanishing points consistent + ground angle ≈90°. That's exactly the 2-VP orthogonality test! And it doesn't need any pitch model/correspondence at all. It's cheap:

- group segments into two direction families by VP (RANSAC)
- get v1 (touchline direction VP), v2 (transverse direction VP)
- f from orthogonality: f² = -(v1-c)·(v2-c)
- residual: how well do the inlier segments actually pass through their VP (angular residual in degrees)
- also the "ground plane 90°" is guaranteed by construction of the orthogonality equation... hmm, so the residual is about VP fit quality, not orthogonality.

Real issue isn't the count per se (2520 fits × cheap score = 770ms). It's that the *winner* is garbage. Why? Because with 640×360 and heavily fragmented lines, the scoring function (mean coverage of touchlines+holdout) is maximized by degenerate homographies that push most model lines out of frame and cover a few strong streaks.

Fix: require the model to be *globally* plausible before scoring:
1. `f` must be recoverable and in [400,3000] — I already check that but *after* selection. Enforce it *during*: skip hypotheses whose `focal_from_h` fails.
2. `cam_h` in [5,60].
3. Both touchlines must be *fully* inside the frame and cover ≥ `touch_cov_min`.
4. The two transverse lines must both be inside the frame.

Enforcing f/h during selection will kill the vast majority of the 2520 hypotheses cheaply and *correctly* (it's a physical constraint, not a fit-to-data trick). The remaining risk is overfitting, but the holdout test covers that.

Let me think about the math. H = K[r1 r2 t]. K⁻¹H = λ[r1 r2 t]. The constraint is r1·r2=0 and |r1|=|r2|. With K=diag(f,f,1) (after T shift), c0=K⁻¹h1, c1=K⁻¹h2. r1·r2 = c0ᵀ K⁻ᵀK⁻¹ c1... 

Standard: (K⁻¹h1)·(K⁻¹h2)=0 gives one equation in u=1/f²; |K⁻¹h1|²=|K⁻¹h2|² gives the second. Both are linear in u. So we get 2 linear equations in ONE unknown u. If both give the same u → consistent (rel≈0). If they disagree → rel large. If a1≈0 (coefficient of u in eq1 is 0) then eq1 is just b1=0, i.e., a pure consistency check with no info on u.

Better approach: rather than fitting a full 4-line homography and requiring focal recovery, use the **vanishing point** approach directly with a strong, physically-motivated criterion:

1. Detect the white-mask segments.
2. RANSAC for vanishing point VP1 (the "length" direction, shared by both touchlines AND halfway line AND goal-area lines... no, halfway is transverse).
3. Transverse lines: perpendicular family in the ground plane — their VP (VP2) must satisfy the orthogonality constraint `v1·v2 + f² = 0` with `f>0` in the range.

That's the classic single-view calibration of a soccer pitch (e.g. Farin, Alvarez). Criterion: two VPs from two line families, orthogonality gives f; then verify with the *known* pitch dimensions (circle centre, penalty spot). This is much more robust than my homography because:
- It doesn't need to know which line is which x — only the family split.
- Orthogonality residual is 1 scalar.
- Holdout: the centre circle must be an ellipse with the right aspect ratio (circle→ellipse constraint gives an independent f estimate!). That's the classic "conics give f" method.

That's a much better criterion and much cheaper. And the discriminative power is real: ad boards give lines that don't split into two orthogonal families.

Let me implement:

```python
def grass_envelope(grass, p):
    """每列的草坪上/下边界 -> 边线的语义先验。

    远端边线 = 广告牌底色带的下沿 = 草坪区域上边界；
    近端边线 = 草坪区域下边界（外侧是跑道）。
    广告牌边缘、跑道线只贴一侧，无法同时满足两条，因此这条先验
    对「长直线」判据是真正的补充，而不是另一个长度阈值。

    返回 (top, bot)，-1 表示该列无效。
    """
    h, w = grass.shape[:2]
    top = np.full(w, -1.0)
    bot = np.full(w, -1.0)
    # 逐列找最长连续草坪段，取它的首末行
    for x in range(w):
        col = grass[:, x] > 0
        if not col.any():
            continue
        # 最长 run
        idx = np.flatnonzero(col)
        splits = np.split(idx, np.flatnonzero(np.diff(idx) > 1) + 1)
        run = max(splits, key=len)
        if len(run) < p.env_min_run:
            continue
        top[x] = float(run[0]); bot[x] = float(run[-1])
    return top, bot
```

```

    rows = np.arange(h)[:, None]
    has = (grass > 0)
    top = np.where(has.any(0), has.argmax(0), -1)
    rev = has[::-1]
    bot = np.where(has.any(0), h - 1 - rev.argmax(0), -1)
    # 中间填充率（防止把观众席上零星的绿像素当成草坪上边界）
    cnt = has.sum(0)
    fill = cnt / np.maximum(1, bot - top + 1)
    bad = (top < 0) | (bot - top < p.env_min_run) | (fill < p.env_fill_min)
    top = top.astype(float); bot = bot.astype(float)
    top[bad] = -1.0; bot[bad] = -1.0
    return top, bot
```

Then a robust line fit through envelope points:

```python
def _fit_env_line(xs, ys, w, min_pts, p):
    """对包络点做一次鲁棒直线拟合，返回线段元组（与 hough_segments 同格式）。"""
    if len(xs) < min_pts:
        return None
    vx = xs.astype(float); vy = ys.astype(float)
    # 迭代重加权：先最小二乘，再剔掉 2.5*MAD 之外的列
    keep = np.ones(vx.size, bool)
    for _ in range(p.env_iters):
        if keep.sum() < min_pts:
            return None
        A = np.stack([vx[keep], np.ones(int(keep.sum()))], 1)
        sol, *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(A, vy[keep], rcond=None)
        r = vy - (sol[0] * vx + sol[1])
        mad = float(np.median(np.abs(r[keep]))) + 1e-6
        keep = np.abs(r) <= max(2.5 * 1.4826 * mad, p.env_tol_px)
    ...
```

Design details:

```python
def grass_envelope(grass, min_col_px=12):
    """逐列草坪的上/下边界行号（-1 = 该列没有足够草坪）。"""
    h, w = grass.shape[:2]
    top = np.full(w, -1, np.int32)
    bot = np.full(w, -1, np.int32)
    for x in range(w):
        col = np.flatnonzero(grass[:, x] > 0)
        if col.size < min_col_px:
            continue
        top[x] = col[0]
        bot[x] = col[-1]
    return top, bot
```

Cost: python loop over 640 columns — measured earlier is fine but let's vectorize with numpy:
```python
any_ = grass > 0
top = np.where(any_.any(0), any_.argmax(0), -1)
bot = np.where(any_.any(0), h - 1 - any_[::-1].argmax(0), -1)
cnt = any_.sum(0)
top = np.where(cnt >= min_col_px, top, -1)
bot = np.where(cnt >= min_col_px, bot, -1)
```
Vectorized, good for the PLA story too (column-wise min/max).

```python
def env_offset(top, bot, seg, n=9):
    """线段中若干采样点到草坪上/下边界的竖直距离的中位数。"""
    xs = np.linspace(seg[0], seg[2], n)
    ys = np.linspace(seg[1], seg[3], n)
    dt, db = [], []
    w = top.size
    for x, y in zip(xs, ys):
        xi = int(round(x))
        if xi < 0 or xi >= w or top[xi] < 0:
            continue
        dt.append(y - top[xi]); db.append(y - bot[xi])
    if not dt:
        return None, None
    return float(np.median(dt)), float(np.median(db))
```

Then synthetic envelope segment:
```python
def env_segment(top, p, which="top"):
    """把草坪上/下边界拟合成一条直线段（鲁棒：要求内点比例够高）。"""
```
Use: collect valid columns, points (x, env[x]). Robust fit: iterate a few times least squares with trimming.

```python
def env_segment(env, p):
    xs = np.flatnonzero(env >= 0)
    if xs.size < p.env_min_cols:
        return None
    ys = env[xs].astype(np.float64)
    X = xs.astype(np.float64)
    keep = np.ones(xs.size, bool)
    a = b = None
    for _ in range(p.env_fit_iter):
        if keep.sum() < p.env_min_cols:
            return None
        A = np.stack([X[keep], np.ones(keep.sum())], 1)
        (a, b), *_ = np.linalg.lstsq(A, ys[keep], rcond=None)
        r = np.abs(ys - (a * X + b))
        s = 1.4826 * np.median(np.abs(r[keep] - np.median(r[keep]))) + 1e-6
        keep = r <= max(p.env_tol_px, 2.5 * s)
    if keep.sum() < p.env_min_frac * xs.size:
        return None
    x0, x1 = float(X[keep].min()), float(X[keep].max())
    ...
```

Let me be careful and precise in the verdict. Key honest statement:
- Geometric criterion (single-focal decomposition of the 4-line homography → orthogonality residual) is measurable and has discriminative power when combined with holdout coverage, but it is *not* self-sufficient.
- At 640×360 broadcast, white-line recall is the binding constraint: on the semantically true touchline, white mask coverage is 0.00–0.76 (median ~0.2), so a 0.55 gate can never be met on both sides.
- Verdict: 部分可行 (partially feasible) — the geometry/self-consistency machinery is sound and implementable, but line detection at this resolution/this camera set is not reliable enough for automatic absolute reference; needs either (a) higher resolution (1280×720 native, ~4× pixels), (b) ROI + temporal accumulation over multiple frames, or (c) semi-automatic initialisation (one-time manual corner pick, then track).

Let me implement `_tab(rows, cols)`:
```python
def _tab(rows, cols):
    """把一批 dict 压成 {cols:[...], rows:[[...]]}，省掉每行重复的键名。"""
    return {"cols": list(cols), "rows": [[_r3(r.get(c)) if isinstance(r.get(c), float) else r.get(c) for c in cols] for r in rows]}
```
